Rational analytic syntomic cohomology
Maximilian Hauck

Abstract
. We define and study the rational analytic syntomification X Syn X^{\mathrm{Syn}} of a partially proper rigid-analytic variety X X over Q p \mathbb{Q}_{p} . We establish Poincaré duality and a theory of first Chern classes for the resulting cohomology theory, identify vector bundles on X Syn X^{\mathrm{Syn}} with de Rham bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve of X ♢ X^{\diamondsuit} and recover several classical comparison theorems in p p -adic Hodge theory. We also develop analogues of our results and constructions over C p \mathbb{C}_{p} .



[intoc]

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14F20, 14F30, 14F40, 14G45

1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a prime number.

1.1.
Motivation and background

Let
X
be a smooth proper variety over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
denote a fixed algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The central question of
p
-adic Hodge theory is the following:

What can be said about the relation between de Rham cohomology
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)

and
p
-adic étale cohomology
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}},\mathbb{Z}_{p})
of
X
?

The first concrete conjectures aimed at answering this question were formulated by Fontaine in
[
24
]
and subsequently studied by many authors, most notably Faltings
[
21
]
, Tsuji
[
53
]
and Nizioł
[
37
]
. Following the introduction of perfectoid spaces in
[
42
]
, similar questions for rigid-analytic varieties instead of algebraic varieties have also been studied in detail, particularly by Scholze
[
43
]
, Colmez–Nizioł
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
and Bosco
[
15
]
. Indeed, the focus of this paper will be this latter case of rigid-analytic varieties.

These developments have spawned a whole zoo of new
p
-adic cohomology theories for algebraic or, more generally, rigid-analytic varieties over
p
-adic fields. On the de Rham side, this includes the so-called log-crystalline cohomology of Hyodo and Kato from
[
32
]
,
[
30
]
, see also
[
4
]
, which is nowadays often simply called
Hyodo–Kato cohomology
and has been adapted to the rigid-analytic setting by Colmez–Nizioł in
[
16
]
, and, in the geometric setting, i.e. over the completion
\mathbb{C}_{p}
of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, the
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-cohomology
introduced in
[
12
, §13]
, see also
[
28
]
. On the étale side, Scholze has introduced a variant of étale cohomology of rigid-analytic varieties called
proétale cohomology
in
[
43
]
. Similarly to the guiding question above, much of
p
-adic Hodge theory is centred around proving comparison theorems between all these cohomology theories and trying to understand how they are related.

The idea behind syntomic cohomology is that there should be a single cohomology theory that mediates all of these comparison theorems. It was first introduced in the algebraic setting by Fontaine–Messing in
[
23
]
and later defined for
p
-adic formal schemes by Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze in
[
13
, §7.4]
as well as for rigid-analytic varieties by Colmez–Nizioł, see
[
16
]
. It is the
p
-adic analogue of Deligne cohomology over the complex numbers and intimately related to
K
-theory and
p
-adic regulators, see e.g.
[
13
, §7.4]
or
[
38
]
, and hence also of interest beyond the scope of
p
-adic comparison theorems.

The major trend in
p
-adic Hodge theory in recent years has been geometrisation. This is the idea that, instead of directly studying some cohomology theory attached to a variety
X
, one constructs a new space attached to
X
which is a geometric avatar of the cohomology theory under consideration; for this process, Bhatt has coined the term
transmutation
. In this approach, one recovers the original cohomology theory by taking cohomology of the structure sheaf on the transmuted space. In the case of de Rham cohomology, this idea goes back to work of Simpson, see
[
50
]
and
[
51
]
, and it has entered the sphere of
p
-adic Hodge theory through recent work of Drinfeld and Bhatt–Lurie on such a “stacky approach” for prismatic and syntomic cohomology of
p
-adic formal schemes, see
[
18
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
11
]
and
[
8
]
.

This geometrisation approach is advantageous for several reasons: Conceptually, it allows one to think about cohomology theories in a geometric way and, in particular, to reinterpret comparison theorems as certain statements about the geometry of the “transmuted” spaces. It also provides a theory of coefficients for a cohomology theory essentially for free: one simply replaces the structure sheaf on the transmuted space by a vector bundle or even a perfect complex. Finally, assuming that one has a six-functor formalism for quasicoherent sheaves on the transmuted spaces, one immediately obtains a full six-functor formalism for the original cohomology theory.

While this picture has been almost fully developed for
p
-adic cohomology theories of
p
-adic formal schemes, much less is known for rigid-analytic varieties. The first steps in this direction have been taken by Rodríguez Camargo, who has introduced the analytic de Rham stack in
[
39
]
, see also the recent update
[
1
]
, and Anschütz–Le Bras–Rodríguez Camargo–Scholze, who have proposed a theory of
analytic prismatisation
for rigid-analytic varieties with
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-coefficients. However, the full picture for syntomic cohomology has been missing. In this paper, we develop the theory of
analytic syntomification
for rigid-analytic varieties with
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-coefficients, making use of the framework of
Gelfand stacks
recently introduced in
[
1
]
. This is a variant of Clausen–Scholze’s analytic stacks tailored to
p
-adic analytic geometry and admits a full six-functor formalism for quasicoherent sheaves.

More precisely, for any partially proper rigid-analytic variety
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, we define its analytic syntomification
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, which is a Gelfand stack over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and taking quasicoherent cohomology on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
yields a notion of rational analytic syntomic cohomology of
X
. For this cohomology theory, we prove Poincaré duality as well as the classical comparison theorems with Hyodo–Kato cohomology and proétale cohomology by studying the geometry of the stack
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. Using similar methods, we also recover the de Rham comparison theorem of Scholze from
[
43
]
. Moreover, if
X
is smooth, we identify the category of vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
with a certain full subcategory of “de Rham” vector bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve of the diamond of
X
. Finally, we also provide variants of these results and constructions for rigid-analytic varieties over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
.

1.2.
Rational analytic syntomification over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

We now briefly describe the construction of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, which works in the generality of
X
being any Gelfand stack over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Namely, similarly to the algebraic story developed by Drinfeld and Bhatt–Lurie in
[
18
]
and
[
8
]
, we will construct an
analytic Nygaardification

X^{\mathrm{N}}
of
X
which receives two maps

j_{\mathrm{dR}},j_{\mathrm{HT}}:X{\Delta}\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{N}}

from the
analytic prismatisation

X{\Delta}
of
X
, the theory of which has been developed by Anschütz–Le Bras–Rodríguez Camargo–Scholze. Then
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is obtained by gluing the two copies of
X{\Delta}
embedded into
X^{\mathrm{N}}
via
j_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
j_{\mathrm{HT}}
.

Crucial for our constructions will be the notion of
nilperfectoid rings
from
[
1
, Def. 4.6.1]
. These are Gelfand rings
A
whose
\dagger
-reduction
\overline{A}\coloneqq A^{\dagger\text{-red}}
is perfectoid, and they form a basis of the
!
-topology on Gelfand rings. In particular, we may define Gelfand stacks by only describing their
A
-valued points for nilperfectoid rings
A
.

With this in mind, let us first recall the definition of the analytic prismatisation
\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in broad strokes. For any nilperfectoid
A
, one constructs a
\dagger
-nilpotent thickening
Y_{A}
of the curve
Y_{\overline{A}}
from
[
1
, §4.4]
. Most importantly, this thickening
Y_{A}
is equipped with a Frobenius
\varphi:Y_{A}\rightarrow Y_{A}
and a map
\iota:\GSpec A\rightarrow Y_{A}
lifting the usual such structures in the perfectoid case
A=\overline{A}
. Then the Gelfand stack
\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}
is obtained by sheafifying the assignment

\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}(A)\coloneqq\{\text{degree $1$ Cartier divisors $D\subseteq Y_{A}$}\}

for nilperfectoids
A
. Here, a Cartier divisor
D\subseteq Y_{A}
is called
of degree
1
if its pullback to
Y_{\overline{A}}
has degree
1
in the sense of
[
25
, Def. II.1.19]
.

To construct
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
, the most important feature of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}
is that it admits a map
\widetilde{\mu}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m})^{\mathrm{dR}}
given by pulling back a degree
1
Cartier divisor along the map
\varphi^{-1}\circ\iota:\GSpec\overline{A}\rightarrow Y_{A}
, which is a Frobenius twist of the map
\iota
from above. In fact, we will show that this may be refined to a map
\widetilde{\mu}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}\rightarrow(\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}
, where
\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the open unit disk and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
is the overconvergent unit torus over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Mimicking an alternative definition of the algebraic filtered prismatisation recently given by Gardner–Madapusi in
[
27
, §6]
, we then define
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
as the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\times(\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t,u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{mult}}

where
(-)^{\mathrm{dR}}
denotes the analytic de Rham stack from
[
1
]
,
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
is the overconvergent unit disk and the map
\mathrm{mult}
is induced by the multiplication map on
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
.

Following Bhatt’s transmutation philosophy, defining
X^{\mathrm{N}}
for any Gelfand stack
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
now comes down to defining a (Gelfand) ring stack
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{N}}
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
; then
X^{\mathrm{N}}
should parametrise maps from
\GSpec\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{N}}(A)
to
X
for any Gelfand ring
A
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
. In fact, we will proceed slightly differently and directly define
\GSpec\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{N}}(A)
. Namely, given an
A
-valued point of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
for
A
nilperfectoid, we will construct a
\dagger
-nilpotent thickening
D^{+}
of the divisor
D\subseteq Y_{A}
classified by the induced map
\GSpec A\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta}
. Then
X^{\mathrm{N}}
is defined as the Gelfand stack over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
obtained as the sheafification of

X^{\mathrm{N}}(\GSpec A\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}})\coloneqq\{\text{maps }D^{+}\rightarrow X\}\;.

Finally, recall the maps
t:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}
and
u:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}\rightarrow(\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}
from above, which induce corresponding maps on
X^{\mathrm{N}}
. Noting that the norms of
t
and
u
are well-defined, it makes sense to consider the loci
\{|t|=1\}
and
\{|u|=1\}
inside
X^{\mathrm{N}}
. We will show that the corresponding substacks of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
are both isomorphic to
X{\Delta}
, and this is what defines the maps
j_{\mathrm{dR}}
and
j_{\mathrm{HT}}
, respectively.

To define analytic syntomic cohomology of any Gelfand stack
X
from here, we construct a line bundle
\mathcal{O}\{1\}
on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
playing the role of a Breuil–Kisin twist and show that it descends to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. Then we set

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\coloneqq R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},\mathcal{O}\{i\})

for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and call it the
rational analytic syntomic cohomology
of
X
of weight
i
. Note that we may just as well take cohomology of any other vector bundle or even perfect complex on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, and this yields a theory of analytic syntomic cohomology with coefficients. In keeping with the algebraic case from
[
8
]
, the category
\mathcal{D}(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
of such coefficients will be called the category of
analytic (prismatic)
F
-gauges
on
X
.

Remark 1.1
.

We briefly sketch how to extend the definition of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to integral coefficients. In fact, the most difficult part of this is to establish a theory of analytic prismatisation with integral coefficients, which is the subject of ongoing joint work of the author with Anschütz–Le Bras–Rodríguez Camargo–Scholze. Once this is in place, the definitions of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
and
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
carry over verbatim. Indeed, in our setting where
X
is a Gelfand stack over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, it turns out that the added complexity of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in the integral coefficient case then purely comes from
X{\Delta}
. This allows one to extend many of our results to the case of integral coefficients.
∎

Remark 1.2
.

The fact that our construction of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
takes as an input a Gelfand stack
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
explains our restriction to rigid-analytic spaces which are partially proper: As explained in
[
1
]
, partially proper rigid spaces are the ones for which the functor to Gelfand stacks is fully faithful. In general, passing to Gelfand stacks identifies a rigid-analytic space with its Huber compactification; e.g., the rigid disk
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}_{p}\langle T\rangle)
is identified with
\Spa(\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, which has an additional rank-
2
-point.
∎

1.3.
Main results

We now state the main results of the paper, which roughly fall into three categories: (1) Interaction of the syntomification with six functors, (2) comparison theorems for
p
-adic cohomology theories and (3) variants in the geometric setting, i.e. over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
.

1.3.1.
Poincaré duality and Chern classes

On the foundational side, we establish basic properties of the functor
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
with respect to the six-functor formalism for quasi-coherent sheaves on Gelfand stacks. The first of these concerns the case
X=\GSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and yields a form of Poincaré duality for cohomology on the stack
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
.

Theorem 1.3

(
6.1
)
.

The map
f:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}\rightarrow\GSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is cohomologically smooth with dualising sheaf
\mathcal{O}\{1\}[3]
, i.e. for any
E\in\Perf(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is an isomorphism

f_{*}(E^{\vee}\{1\})[3]\cong(f_{!}E)^{\vee}\;.

Remark 1.4
.

Normally, one would expect the dualising sheaf to be concentrated in degree
-2
instead of
-3
, e.g. by comparing with the duality obtained by Bhatt–Lurie for the algebraic syntomification of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in
[
8
, Cor. 6.4.6]
or with local Tate duality for Galois representations. The extra shift here is due to the fact that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
has an extra “topological dimension” that roughly arises from the Betti stack of an interval. However, this “extra interval” does not affect cohomology of perfect complexes on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
by contractibility.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

We do not expect
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to be cohomologically proper (or even prim). Roughly speaking, this is due to the fact that it is “open at
\{p=0\}
in the geometry”. More to the point, there should be a variant of analytic syntomification which also allows for inputs with integral geometry; then we expect
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to be weakly cohomologically proper and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to be an open substack inside
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
.
∎

The next result concerns the relative situation, i.e. it is about induced maps between syntomifications.

Theorem 1.6

(
6.14
,
6.19
)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a map of derived Berkovich spaces in the sense of
[
1
, §4.3]
.

(a)

If
f
is rigid smooth, then the induced morphism
f^{\mathrm{Syn}}:X^{\mathrm{Syn}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is cohomologically smooth. If
f
is additionally pure of relative dimension
d
, the dualising sheaf of
f^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is given by
\mathcal{O}\{d\}[2d]
.

(b)

If
f
is locally of finite presentation and quasicompact, the induced morphism
f^{\mathrm{Syn}}:X^{\mathrm{Syn}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathrm{Syn}}
is weakly cohomologically proper.

In particular, if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a smooth proper map of partially proper rigid spaces pure of relative dimension
d
, then
f^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{*}
preserves perfect complexes and we have

f^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{*}(E^{\vee}\{d\})[2d]\cong(f^{\mathrm{Syn}}_{*}E)^{\vee}

for any
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
.

Finally, we show that syntomic cohomology of derived Berkovich spaces admits a strong theory of first Chern classes in the sense of
[
55
, §5]
. More precisely, this means the following:

Proposition 1.7

(
6.23
)
.

For any derived Berkovich space
X
, there is a natural morphism

c_{1}^{\mathrm{Syn}}:R\Gamma(X_{\mathrm{Berk}\text{-}\mathrm{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{G}_{m})[1]\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},\mathcal{O}\{1\}[2])\;,

where the left-hand side denotes the cohomology of
\mathbb{G}_{m}[1]
on the Berkovich étale site of
X
. Moreover, the projective bundle formula holds: for any
d\geq 1
and any derived Berkovich space
X
, the induced morphism

\sum_{0\leq k\leq d}(c_{1}^{\mathrm{Syn}})^{k}\{d-k\}[2d-2k]:\bigoplus_{0\leq k\leq d}\mathcal{O}\{d-k\}[2d-2k]\rightarrow f_{*}^{\mathrm{Syn}}\mathcal{O}\{d\}[2d]

is an isomorphism, where
f:\mathbb{P}^{d}_{X}\rightarrow X
is the projection.

1.3.2.
Comparison theorems

By studying the geometry of the syntomification, we can recover many of the classical comparison theorems in the
p
-adic Hodge theory of rigid-analytic varieties and, in many cases, upgrade them to coefficients in arbitrary perfect complexes on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
. This will culminate in a description of the category
\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
for a smooth partially proper rigid space
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in terms of de Rham bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve of
X^{\diamondsuit}
. In particular, this shows that vector bundle analytic
F
-gauges are actually very classical objects in
p
-adic Hodge theory.

We begin with Scholze’s de Rham comparison theorem from
[
43
]
. For this, we first use the geometry of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to construct a functor

(1.3.1)

\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})\rightarrow\Perf(X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})

for any smooth partially proper rigid space
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Here,
X^{\mathrm{dR},+}
denotes the filtered analytic de Rham stack of
X
from
[
39
, Def. 5.2.2]
, perfect complexes on which identify with filtered perfect complexes with connection on
X
.

Theorem 1.8

(
7.1
)
.

The functor

\{\text{filtered vector bundles with connection on $X$}\}\rightarrow\{\text{$\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}$-local systems on $X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}$}\}

obtained by restricting (
1.3.1
) to
\Vect(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})
identifies with Scholze’s functor

(\Fil^{\bullet}E,\nabla)\mapsto\Fil^{0}(E\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR}})^{\nabla=0}

from
[
43
, §7]
.

Using this, the version of the de Rham comparison theorem we recover takes the following form:

Theorem 1.9

(
7.9
)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a smooth proper morphism of smooth partially proper rigid spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathbb{L}
be a de Rham local system on
X
with associated filtered vector bundle with connection
E\in\Vect(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})
. Then the pushforward
f^{\mathrm{dR},+}_{*}E\in\mathcal{D}(Y^{\mathrm{dR},+})
is perfect and its image under the functor (
1.3.1
) is given by
f_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t},*}\mathbb{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
. In particular, for any smooth proper rigid space
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, there is an isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{\mathbb{C}_{p}},\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}

compatible with the
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-actions and the filtrations.

We can also recover the classical comparison theorems of Colmez–Nizioł between syntomic cohomology and Hyodo–Kato cohomology or proétale cohomology, respectively, from
[
16
]
. In fact, we are able to extend them to coefficients in an arbitrary perfect analytic
F
-gauge and reinterpret them as descriptions of the full category of perfect analytic
F
-gauges in terms of coefficients for Hyodo–Kato cohomology or proétale cohomology, respectively.

Namely, in the Hyodo–Kato case, we construct realisation functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HK}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{HK}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{dR},+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{dR}}}

for any Gelfand stack
X
, where we recall that
X^{\mathrm{HK}}
is the
Hyodo–Kato stack
of
X
from
[
1
, §6]
. Then we prove:

Theorem 1.10

(
8.3
)
.

Let
X
be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})\cong\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HK}})\times_{\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR}})}\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR},+})

induced by the realisation functors
T_{\mathrm{HK}}
and
T_{\mathrm{dR},+}
. In particular, for any
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HK}},T_{\mathrm{HK}}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Remark 1.11
.

Specialising the result above to the case where
E=\mathcal{O}\{i\}
is a Breuil–Kisin twist recovers the more classical statement
[
16
, Thm. 1.1.(4)]
. In particular, this shows that our analytic syntomic cohomology coincides with Colmez–Nizioł syntomic cohomology if we take coefficients in a Breuil–Kisin twist.
∎

We proceed similarly in the proétale case: Here, we construct realisation functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}}

for any Gelfand stack
X
, where
X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}
was defined in
[
2
]
and computes proétale cohomology of
X
as shown in loc. cit. while
X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}
and
X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}
are certain closed substacks of
X^{\mathrm{N}}
to be defined in §
4.2
below. Then our result reads as follows:

Theorem 1.12

(
9.3
)
.

Let
X
be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})\cong\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}})\times_{\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger})}\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+})

induced by the realisation functors
T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}
and
T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}
. In particular, for any
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
, there is a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}},T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}(E))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

The conclusion above does not yet provide an immediate comparison between syntomic cohomology and proétale cohomology. However, analysing the map

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}(E))\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}(E))

in the diagram above, we obtain the following variant (see §
4.3
for the definition of Hodge–Tate weights in this context):

Theorem 1.13

(
9.4
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. If
E\in\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})
is a vector bundle analytic
F
-gauge with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-i
for some
i\geq 0
, then the natural morphism

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}},E)\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}},T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}(E))

is an isomorphism on
\tau^{\leq i}
and induces an injection on
H^{i+1}
. In particular, for
E=\mathcal{O}\{i\}
, we have

\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\cong\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\;.

Remark 1.14
.

Once a robust theory of analytic syntomification with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-coefficients is set up, we expect that the statements as well as the proofs of
1.12
and
1.13
carry over almost verbatim to the integral coefficient case. In contrast, this should
not
be expected for
1.10
as this is an inherently rational statement.
∎

Finally, we can use the results above to explicitly describe the category of vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
for smooth partially proper rigid spaces
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In particular, this allows us to relate the analytic
F
-gauge coefficients that are allowed in the comparison theorems above to more classical objects in
p
-adic Hodge theory. We start with the case
X=\GSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Theorem 1.15

(
8.9
)
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\Vect(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}})\cong\Vect^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathrm{FF}_{\Spd\mathbb{Q}_{p}})

between vector bundles on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
and
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-equivariant vector bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve which are de Rham in the sense of
[
22
, Def. 15.12]
. Moreover, if
V
and
W
are de Rham representations of
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, then

\RHom_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}}(V,W)\cong\RHom_{\Rep^{\mathrm{dR}}(\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})}(V,W)\;.

In particular, we have

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}},V)\cong H^{1}_{g}(\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V)\;.

In the above theorem, the group
H^{1}_{g}
was defined by Bloch–Kato in
[
9
]
and coincides with the subspace of
H^{1}
spanned by extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
by
V
which are de Rham. This parallels the description of reflexive sheaves on the algebraic syntomification of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in terms of lattices in crystalline representations of
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
given by Bhatt–Lurie in
[
8
, Thm. 6.6.13]
and their recovery of the Bloch–Kato–Selmer group
H^{1}_{f}
in terms of
H^{1}
on the algebraic syntomification of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
from
[
8
, Prop. 6.7.3]
.

Finally, we are able to extend the above result to arbitrary smooth partially proper rigid spaces
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In particular, this relates the category of vector bundle analytic
F
-gauges to the category of de Rham local systems on
X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}
, which are a more classical candidate for a category of “universal coefficients” for
p
-adic cohomology theories attached to
X
.

Theorem 1.16

(
9.16
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})\cong\Vect^{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathrm{FF}_{X^{\diamondsuit}})\;,

where the right-hand side denotes the full subcategory of vector bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve of
X^{\diamondsuit}
which are de Rham in the sense of
9.14
. In particular, there is a fully faithful embedding

\Loc^{\mathrm{dR}}(X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Q}_{p})\hookrightarrow\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}})

of the category of de Rham local systems on
X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}
into the category of vector bundle analytic
F
-gauges on
X
.

Remark 1.17
.

There should also be a variant of the above result with integral coefficients. Namely, we expect that vector bundles on the integral analytic syntomification of a partially proper rigid space
X
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
are equivalent to lattices in de Rham local systems on
X_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}
, with the proof being mostly analogous to the one we give for
1.16
. Indeed, one should think of the passage to integral coefficients as adding a slope zero condition on the Fargues–Fontaine curve, which explains the fact the we only see honest local systems in the integral coefficient case.
∎

1.3.3.
Syntomic cohomology over
\mathbb{C}_{p}

Finally, we develop analogues of the above results and constructions for rigid-analytic varieties over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. For this, the key is to replace
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
by an appropriate base change, which we denote by
X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}}
for any Gelfand stack
X
over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. As a warning, let us already point out that the resulting syntomic cohomology over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
we obtain is
different
from the one defined by Colmez–Nizioł, see
1.22
below.

As the base change that yields
X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}}
does not change the overall geometry of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
, the proofs of the comparison theorems above mostly go through unchanged. The main difference is that we now see a new stack appearing, which we call the filtered
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}
-stack
of
X
and denote by
X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}}
. The main part of the work lies in identifying the base change of the cohomology of the structure sheaf on this stack to
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
with the
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-cohomology
R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})
of
X
from
[
12
, §13]
or
[
28
]
, which we recall also has a natural filtration.

Theorem 1.18

(
10.8
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then there is a natural isomorphism

\Fil^{\bullet}R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},\mathcal{O})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\cong\Fil^{\bullet}R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\;,

where the left-hand side denotes the base change of the filtered object

\dots\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},\mathcal{O}\langle-(i+1)\rangle)\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},\mathcal{O}\langle-i\rangle)\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},\mathcal{O}\langle-(i-1)\rangle)\rightarrow\dots

from the overconvergent de Rham period ring
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}
to
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
.

Remark 1.19
.

Using this stacky approach to
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-cohomology, we are also able to give straightforward proofs of the basic theorems relating
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-cohomology and de Rham cohomology in
10.10
. Namely, for any smooth partially proper rigid space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
, we have

R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}\mathbb{C}_{p}\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/\mathbb{C}_{p})

and, if
X
is in addition qcqs and admits a model
\overline{X}
over some finite extension
K
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then

R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\cong R\Gamma_{\mathrm{dR}}(\overline{X}/K)\otimes_{K}B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}\;.\qed

Then the first comparison theorem we can recover is the de Rham comparison theorem over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
from
[
12
, Thm. 13.1]
, which takes the following shape:

Theorem 1.20

(
10.12
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth proper rigid space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then there is a natural isomorphism

R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}\cong R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\otimes_{B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}}B_{\mathrm{dR}}

compatible with the filtrations.

We also obtain geometric analogues of the comparison theorems between syntomic cohomology and Hyodo–Kato cohomology or proétale cohomology, respectively:

Theorem 1.21

(
10.14
)
.

Let
X
be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})\cong\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HK}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})\times_{\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR}/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}})}\Perf(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}})\;.

In particular, for any
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})
, there is a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HK}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{HK}}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR},+/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},T_{\mathrm{dR},+}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{dR}/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+,\dagger}},T_{\mathrm{dR}}(E))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Corollary 1.22

(
10.16
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper qcqs rigid space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X/\mathbb{C}_{p},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R\Gamma_{\mathrm{HK}}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{un}}}B_{\log})^{\varphi=p^{i},N=0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fil^{i}R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X/B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+})\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}

where
R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X/\mathbb{C}_{p},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))
denotes the cohomology of
\mathcal{O}\{i\}
on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}}
and
B_{\log}
is the period ring from
[
22
, Def. 10.3.1]
.

Comparing the above corollary to
[
16
, Thm. 1.1.(4)]
, we see that, over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
, our analytic syntomic cohomology does
not
coincide with Colmez–Nizioł syntomic cohomology due to the appearance of the period ring
B_{\log}
in place of
B_{\mathrm{st}}^{+}
. Instead, by
[
15
, Thm. 6.3]
, we recover the
syntomic Fargues–Fontaine cohomology
of Bosco.

Theorem 1.23

(
10.19
)
.

Let
X
be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})\cong\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})\times_{\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger/\mathbb{C}_{p}})}\Perf(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+/\mathbb{C}_{p}})\;.

In particular, for any
E\in\Perf(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})
, there is a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}(E))\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;.}}

Again, an analysis of the map

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}(E))\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{HT},\dagger}(E))

enables us to give a more direct comparison between syntomic and proétale cohomology.

Theorem 1.24

(
10.21
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid space over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
. If
E\in\Vect(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}})
is a vector bundle analytic
F
-gauge on
X
relative to
\mathbb{C}_{p}
with Hodge–Tate weights all at most
-i
for some
i\geq 0
, then the natural morphism

R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Syn}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},E)\rightarrow R\Gamma(X^{\mathrm{Div}^{1}/\mathbb{C}_{p}},T_{\mathrm{Div}^{1}}(E))

is an isomorphism on
\tau^{\leq i}
and induces an injection on
H^{i+1}
. In particular, for
E=\mathcal{O}\{i\}
, we obtain

\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{Syn}}(X/\mathbb{C}_{p},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\cong\tau^{\leq i}R\Gamma_{\mathrm{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathbb{Q}_{p}(i))\;.

1.4.
Organisation of the paper

In §2, we mostly review some aspects of the theory of Gelfand stacks and their quasicoherent sheaves. The main novelty here is a spreading out property for perfect complexes on a certain class of Gelfand stacks which we call “nicely coverable”. This will be proved in §2.3 and is one of the main ingredients in our derivation of comparison theorems from the geometry of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
later on. We also establish a version of the Rees equivalence in the analytic setting, see §2.4.

We then move on to reviewing the theory of rational analytic prismatisation as developed by Anschütz–Le Bras–Rodríguez Camargo–Scholze in §3. While everything we discuss there is due to them, we have still chosen to be as detailed as possible since a written account of the theory was not yet available at the time of writing.

With these preliminaries out of the way, we can define the analytic Nygaardification and the analytic syntomification in §4. In §4.2, we study the basic geometry of the analytic Nygaardification and how it relates to other stacks attached to
p
-adic cohomology theories. Finally, we establish two technical properties of the Nygaardification: First, we prove in §4.4 that the functor
X\mapsto X^{\mathrm{N}}
on derived Berkovich spaces is compatible with étale localisation, which will be the key to almost all of our dévissage arguments in the rest of the paper. Second, in §4.5, we show that, after restricting to a certain locus, the stack
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
is nicely coverable in the sense of §2.3. This will be crucial for us to later be able to apply the spreading out property for perfect complexes proved earlier.

In §5, we give explicit presentations for some Nygaardifications as quotient stacks. Namely, we start with the case
X=\GSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in §5.1 and then move on to the case
X=\mathbb{G}_{m}
in §5.2. These presentations will be key to our proofs of the cohomological smoothness and properness results for the syntomification. Then we study a certain closed substack of
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
that we call
X^{\mathrm{HT},\dagger,+}
in more detail in §5.3 by not only giving an explicit presentation as a quotient, but also describing its category of perfect complexes and their cohomology in the case where
X
admits a Berkovich étale map to the
n
-dimensional overconvergent unit torus
\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{n}
. This will be the crucial ingredient for both the derivation of the comparison between syntomic and proétale cohomology in low degrees and the description of vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in terms of de Rham vector bundles on
\mathrm{FF}_{X^{\diamondsuit}}
later on.

Using the results of §5, we will be able to establish our results concerning the properties of the syntomification with respect to six functors in §6. Here we first prove cohomological smoothness of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in §6.1 and then move on to the relative case
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}\rightarrow Y^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in §6.2. Finally, §6.3 is devoted to establishing the existence of a strong theory of first Chern classes for syntomic cohomology of derived Berkovich spaces in the sense of
[
55
, §5]
.

In §7, we start proving comparison theorems. Namely, we discuss how to interpret Scholze’s results from
[
43
]
in terms of the syntomification by first giving an alternative construction of his functor from filtered vector bundles with connection to
\mathbb{B}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-local systems in §7.1 and then reproving the de Rham comparison theorem in §7.2.

We then move on to the comparison between syntomic cohomology and Hyodo–Kato cohomology in §8. We first discuss how to describe the category of perfect complexes on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in terms of perfect complexes on the Hyodo–Kato stack and on the filtered de Rham stack of
X
in §8.1. Then we use this to relate vector bundles on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
to
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-equivariant bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve which are de Rham and to recover Bloch–Kato’s
H^{1}_{g}
via the syntomification in §8.2.

Subsequently, we discuss the comparison between syntomic and proétale cohomology in §9. The method here will be closely analogous to the one from §8 and hence we content ourselves with outlining the steps of the proof in §9.1. Instead, the focus of this section will be §9.2, where we put this together with our results from §5.3 in order to prove the description of vector bundles on
X^{\mathrm{Syn}}
in terms of de Rham bundles on
\mathrm{FF}_{X^{\diamondsuit}}
.

Finally, we discuss syntomic cohomology over
\mathbb{C}_{p}
in §10, for which we will first introduce the relevant base changes in §10.1. The main part of this section is the stacky approach to
B_{\mathrm{dR}}^{+}
-cohomology, which we develop in §10.2. Afterwards, the proofs of the comparison theorems go through just as in the arithmetic case and hence we only give outlines in §10.3.

Notations and conventions

Throughout, we use the solid analytic ring structure on
\mathbb{Z}
and, in particular, all tensor products as well as all abelian groups are implicitly understood to be solid. Moreover, all functors, e.g. tensor products or pushforwards, will always implicitly be derived and hence, in particular,
\otimes
always denotes a solid derived tensor product.

All Gelfand
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebras will be assumed to be separable and qfd in the sense of
[
1
, Def. 4.2.6]
and
[
1
, Def. 4.2.14]
. In particular, all our Gelfand stacks will implicitly be qfd.

For any Gelfand
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra
A
and
r\geq 0
, we write

A\langle T\rangle_{\leq r}\coloneqq A\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{\leq r}\;,\hskip 8.5359ptA\{T\}^{\dagger}\coloneqq A\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{\leq 0}\;,

where
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{\leq 0}
is the ring of overconvergent functions on the closed disk of radius
r
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
from
[
1
, Def. 2.2.3]
. Moreover, we will write
\overline{A}\coloneqq A^{\dagger\text{-red}}
to denote
\dagger
-reductions in the sense of
[
1
, Def. 2.2.19]
.

The term “rational localisation” will always refer to a rational localisation on the Berkovich spectrum and hence rational localisations are always overconvergent; e.g., we have

(\{|f|\leq 1\}\subseteq\GSpec A)\coloneqq\GSpec A\langle f\rangle_{\leq 1}

for any Gelfand ring
A
and any
f\in A
. We warn the reader that
A\langle f\rangle_{\leq 1}\neq A\langle f\rangle
, i.e. this usage of the term “rational localisation” differs from the terminology commonly used in the context of adic spaces.

Throughout, there will be a number of different twists that play a role: The Tate twist is always denoted by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1)
and the Breuil–Kisin twist (on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}{\Delta},\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{N}}
or
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{Syn}}
) is denoted by
\mathcal{O}\{1\}
. Finally, we have chosen the slightly nonstandard notation
\mathcal{O}\langle-1\rangle
for the tautological normed line bundle on the classifying stack
*/\overline{\mathbb{T}}
of the overconvergent unit torus.

2.
Preliminaries on Gelfand stacks

We start by recording some facts about Gelfand rings and Gelfand stacks over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. With the exception of §
2.3
, most of the material presented should be considered standard and, in particular, all the definitions are taken from
[
1
]
.

2.1.
Quasicoherent sheaves and perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks

Recall the definition of quasicoherent sheaves on Gelfand stacks.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X
be a Gelfand stack. Its (derived) category of
quasicoherent sheaves
is defined as

\mathcal{D}(X)\coloneqq\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{\GSpec A\rightarrow X}\mathcal{D}(A)\;,

where the transition maps are given by pullback and
\mathcal{D}(A)
denotes the category of
A
-modules in
\mathcal{D}_{\square}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
for any Gelfand
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebra
A
.

We also recall that the functor

\mathrm{GelfStk}\rightarrow\mathrm{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}\;,\hskip 8.5359ptX\mapsto\mathcal{D}(X)

promotes to a full six functor formalism in which all morphisms between affine Gelfand stacks

f:\GSpec B=Y\rightarrow X=\GSpec A

are
!
-able and weakly cohomologically proper, i.e. there is an equivalence
f_{!}\cong f_{*}
. Nevertheless, throughout most of this paper, we will mostly be considering just perfect complexes instead of all quasicoherent sheaves. For this, we recall that this is a well-behaved notion in Gelfand stacks.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
be a Gelfand stack. The category
\Perf(X)
of
perfect complexes
on
X
is defined as the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(X)
consisting of dualisable objects.

The association
X\mapsto\Perf(X)
automatically satisfies descent, but the above definition has the downside that dualisability in
\mathcal{D}(A)
is a priori hard to control for arbitrary analytic rings
A
. This is not the case for Gelfand rings:

Proposition 2.3
.

For any Gelfand ring
A
, we have
\Perf(A)\cong\Perf(A(*))
, where the right-hand side denotes the usual category of perfect complexes over the discrete animated ring
A(*)
. Moreover, the association

A\mapsto\Perf^{[a,b]}(A)\;,

where the right-hand side denotes the full subcategory of perfect complexes over
A(*)
with Tor-amplitude in
[a,b]
extends uniquely to a sheaf of categories on
\mathrm{GelfStk}
.

Proof.

See
[
1
, Prop. 3.3.5]
and
[
1
, Lem. 4.2.11]
.
∎

In particular, we also obtain a meaningful notion of vector bundles on Gelfand stacks:

Definition 2.4
.

For any Gelfand stack
X
and integers
a\leq b
, we define

\Perf^{[a,b]}(X)\coloneqq\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{\GSpec A\rightarrow X}\Perf^{[a,b]}(A)\;,

where the limit is taken along pullback functors. The category
\Vect(X)
of
vector bundles
on
X
is defined by
\Vect(X)\coloneqq\Perf^{[0,0]}(X)
.

2.2.
Betti stacks and their perfect complexes

Recall that there is a functor

\operatorname{Pro}{N}(\mathrm{Fin})\rightarrow\mathrm{GelfRing}^{\mathrm{op}}\;,\hskip 8.5359ptS\mapsto\GSpec C^{\mathrm{sm}}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{p})

taking any light profinite set
S
to the algebra of locally constant
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-valued functions on
S
.

Proposition 2.5
.

The above functor uniquely extends to a colimit-preserving functor

(-)^{\mathrm{Betti}}:\widehat{\Shv}(\mathrm{Pro}_{\mathbb{N}}(\mathrm{Fin}))\rightarrow\mathrm{GelfStk}

from hypersheaves on light profinite sets to Gelfand stacks. If
S
is a finite-dimensional metrisable compact Hausdorff space (seen as a light condensed set), then there is an equivalence

\mathcal{D}(X\times S^{\mathrm{Betti}})\cong\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))

for any Gelfand stack
X
, where the right-hand side denotes the category of
\mathcal{D}(X)
-valued sheaves on
S
.

Proof.

See
[
1
, Lem. 4.2.9]
and
[
46
, Cor. II.1.2]
.
∎

We immediately warn the reader the we will usually
not
write the superscript
(-)^{\mathrm{Betti}}
when it is clear from the context; e.g., we might write expressions like
X\times[0,1]
for a Gelfand stack
X
and then it should be clear that
[0,1]
is seen as a Gelfand stack via its Betti stack. We next want to characterise perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks of the form
X\times S^{\mathrm{Betti}}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
X
be any Gelfand stack and
S
a CW complex. Then

\Perf(X\times S)\cong\LocConst(S,\Perf(X))\cong\Fun(\Sing(S),\Perf(X))\;,

where the middle term is the full subcategory of
\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))
consisting of locally constant sheaves valued in
\Perf(X)
and
\Sing(S)
denotes the singular complex of
S
, which we view as an
\infty
-groupoid.

Proof.

The second equivalence is a consequence of the results in
[
34
, App. A]
. Since

\Perf(X\times S)\cong\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{\GSpec A\rightarrow X}\Perf(\GSpec A\times S)

by descent and similarly for
\Perf(X)
, proving that the two outer categories above are equivalent reduces to the affine case
X=\GSpec A
. Moreover, the terms
\Perf(X\times S)
and
\Fun(\Sing(S),\Perf(X))
take colimits in
S
to limits (in the former case this is because colimits are universal in
\infty
-topoi) and hence we may reduce to the case of a finite CW complex (even to the case of
S
being an
n
-dimensional unit ball or an
n
-dimensional unit sphere).

Having made these reductions, we are now going to prove the first of the equivalences in the statement, which then implies the second by the previous paragraph. For this, recall from
2.5
above that there is an equivalence

\mathcal{D}(X\times S)\cong\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))

and hence, by definition of perfect complexes as dualisable objects, we have to show that the dualisable objects of
\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))
are exactly the locally constant sheaves with dualisable stalks. However, noting that dualisability of
\mathcal{F}\in\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))
is local on
S
because it is equivalent to the canonical map
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{F},1)\otimes\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
being an isomorphism, we easily see that
\mathcal{F}
is dualisable whenever it is locally constant with dualisable stalks.

For the converse, assume that
\mathcal{F}
is dualisable. By passing to stalks, we see that this in particular implies that all stalks of
\mathcal{F}
are dualisable, and hence we only need to show that
\mathcal{F}
is locally constant. As
S
is compact Hausdorff, taking global sections commutes with filtered colimits and thus the unit
1\in\Shv(S,\mathcal{D}(X))
is compact. Since
\Hom(\mathcal{F},-)\cong\Hom(1,(-)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
, this implies that
\mathcal{F}
is compact as well. However, noting that
\mathcal{D}(X)=\mathcal{D}(A)
is compactly generated and hence dualisable,
\mathcal{F}
being compact implies that it is locally constant by
[
19
, Prop. 6.15]
, so we are done.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
X
be any Gelfand stack and
S
a CW complex. If
S
is contractible, then

\Perf(X\times S)\cong\Perf(X)

via pullback along the projection
X\times S\rightarrow X
.

We end our discussion of Betti stacks by recording the following gluing lemma for later use.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
X\rightarrow(0,\infty)
be a morphism of Gelfand stacks and denote the preimage of any interval
I\subseteq(0,\infty)
by
X_{I}
. For any
r\in(0,\infty)
, the natural map induces an isomorphism

X_{(0,r]}\coprod_{X_{[r,r]}}X_{[r,\infty)}\xrightarrow{\cong}X\;.

Similar statements hold for other intervals in place of
(0,\infty)
and for other decompositions of such intervals.

Proof.

We start by noting that

{(0,r]}\coprod_{[r,r]}{[r,\infty)}\xrightarrow{\cong}(0,\infty)

via the natural map. Since colimits are universal in
\infty
-topoi, i.e. they commute with pullbacks, pulling back this isomorphism along
X\rightarrow(0,\infty)
yields the claim.
∎

2.3.
Spreading out perfect complexes

Before we move on, we record a technical result concerning a certain “spreading out” property of perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks. We begin by establishing some terminology.

Definition 2.9
.

A
!
-hypercover
X_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
of Gelfand stacks is called
admissible
if the natural map

\mathcal{D}(X)\rightarrow\operatornamewithlimits{lim}\mathcal{D}(X_{\bullet})

is an equivalence and the same holds for any base change.

Example 2.10
.

By definition of the
!
-topology, the Čech nerve of any surjection
Y\rightarrow X
of Gelfand stacks is an admissible
!
-hypercover.
∎

Definition 2.11
.

A Gelfand stack
X
is called
nicely coverable
if there is an admissible
!
-hypercover

\GSpec A_{\bullet}\rightarrow X

of
X
by affine Gelfand stacks such that all
A_{\bullet}
are
n
-truncated animated rings for some
n\geq 0
. Any such
!
-hypercover is called a
nice
hypercover of
X
.

Verifying that certain explicit Gelfand stacks of interest are nicely coverable will be the most technical part of our arguments. Thus, it will be useful to establish some permanence properties of the notion of nice coverability. In the following, when we speak about “flatness”, we will always mean flatness with respect to the solid tensor product.

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider a morphism
X\rightarrow Y
of Gelfand stacks and assume that
X
is nicely coverable. If
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
is an affine morphism of bounded flat dimension, then
X^{\prime}\coloneqq X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
is nicely coverable as well.

Proof.

If
\GSpec A_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
is a nice hypercover such that all
A_{\bullet}
are
n
-truncated and the flat dimension of
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
is bounded by
m\geq 0
, then
\GSpec A_{\bullet}\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
will be an admissible hypercover where all
\GSpec A_{\bullet}\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
are affine and
(n+m)
-truncated.
∎

The next lemma says that being nicely coverable is basically local for admissible hypercovers up to an additional uniformity assumption.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
X_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
be an admissible
!
-hypercover of Gelfand stacks. If there is some fixed
n\geq 0
such that any
X_{\bullet}
admits an admissible
!
-hypercover by
n
-truncated affine Gelfand stacks, then
X
is nicely coverable.

Proof.

For each
j\geq 0
, let
\GSpec A_{\bullet}^{j}\rightarrow X_{j}
be admissible hypercovers by affine
n
-truncated Gelfand stacks. Considering

B_{\bullet}\coloneqq A_{0}^{\bullet}\times A_{1}^{\bullet-1}\times\dots\times A_{\bullet}^{0}\;,

we see that
\GSpec B_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
is an admissible hypercover since
\GSpec B_{\bullet}=\bigsqcup_{i+j=\bullet}\GSpec A_{i}^{j}
and hence

\operatornamewithlimits{lim}\mathcal{D}(B_{\bullet})\cong\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{i,j}\mathcal{D}(A_{i}^{j})\cong\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{j}\mathcal{D}(X_{j})\cong\mathcal{D}(X)\;.

Clearly, all
B_{\bullet}
are
n
-truncated since the
A_{i}^{j}
are.
∎

As nice coverability is intimately related to flatness questions, it will be convenient to record the following two flatness lemmas before we move on.

Lemma 2.14
.

For each
r\geq\varepsilon>0
, the
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(r))
-algebra
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon))
has flat dimension at most
1
with respect to the solid tensor product, where
\overline{\mathbb{D}}(r)
denotes the overconvergent closed disk of radius
r
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(r))
is an idempotent
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]
-algebra and hence it suffices to check that
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon))
has flat dimension at most
1
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]
. Furthermore, as
\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon))
is a colimit of Tate algebras, we are reduced to checking that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle
has flat dimension at most
1
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]
. However, recall that
\prod_{\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is a flat
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module and hence

\mathbb{Z}_{p}\langle U\rangle=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\\
f(n)\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\prod_{\mathbb{N}}p^{f(n)}\mathbb{Z}_{p}U^{n}

is also flat over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. This implies that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]\langle U\rangle
is flat over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]
by base change and thus

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]\langle U\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]\langle U\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T-U}

is a flat resolution of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T]
, whence the claim. (Note that one could also have deduced the flatness of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle U\rangle
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
from
2.15
below.)
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
K
be a nonarchimedean local field. Then any quasiseparated solid
K
-vector space is flat.

Proof.

See
[
14
, Lem. A.28]
.
∎

The following lemma shows that nice coverability is preserved under passing to finite étale covers and rational localisations.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of Gelfand stacks which becomes finite étale after pulling back to any totally disconnected nilperfectoid
\GSpec A\rightarrow Y
over
Y
. If
Y
is nicely coverable, then
X
is nicely coverable as well. Moreover, the same conclusion holds if we replace “finite étale” by “rational localisation”.

Proof.

Let
Y_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y
be an admissible hypercover by
n
-truncated affine Gelfand stacks. We claim that there is a
!
-cover
\GSpec A\rightarrow Y_{0}
with
A
strictly totally disconnected nilperfectoid which is in addition flat. Indeed, by examining the proof of
[
1
, Prop. 6.4.6]
, this comes down to checking that the map
\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\mathrm{cycl}}\langle T_{i}^{1/p^{\infty}}:i\geq 1\rangle
is flat, which we may in turn reduce to checking that the maps
\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{i}:i\geq 1\rangle
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T^{1/p}\rangle
are flat. For the latter map, this is clear as
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T^{1/p}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle[T^{1/p}]
is a free module of rank
p
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle
. For the former map, note that it suffices to check flatness of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{i}:i\geq 1\rangle
for each
n\geq 1
, but this follows by base change from flatness of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T_{i}:i>n\rangle
over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, which is in turn a consequence of
2.15
.

Picking a flat
!
-cover
\GSpec A\rightarrow Y_{0}
with
A
strictly totally disconnected nilperfectoid as above, by
2.12
, the base change of the hypercover
Y_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y
along the map
\GSpec A\rightarrow Y_{0}
yields a nice hypercover of
Y
whose zeroth term is totally disconnected nilperfectoid. In other words, we may assume that
Y_{0}
is totally disconnected nilperfectoid and then the base change
X_{0}\coloneqq Y_{0}\times_{Y}X
is finite étale over
Y_{0}
by assumption. Consequently,
X_{\bullet}\coloneqq Y_{\bullet}\times_{Y}X
is finite étale over
Y_{\bullet}
. Thus, each
X_{\bullet}
is affine and
n
-truncated as well; moreover, as admissible hypercovers are stable under base change, we conclude that
X_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
is a nice hypercover and this finishes the proof in the finite étale case.

In the case where
X\rightarrow Y
becomes a rational localisation after base change to any strictly totally disconnected nilperfectoid over
Y
, one argues similarly: As the base change
X\times_{Y}\GSpec A
is defined by finitely many inequalities of the form
|f|\leq 1
or
|f|\geq 1
inside
\GSpec A
, the map
X\times_{Y}\GSpec A\rightarrow\GSpec A
has finite flat dimension by
2.14
and thus nice hypercovers are preserved by base change along this map by
2.12
.
∎

Finally, the last missing definition in order for us to be able to state the desired spreading out property is the following:

Definition 2.17
.

Let
X
be a Gelfand stack. An
overconvergent normed divisor

Z\subseteq X
is a closed substack of
X
arising from a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*/\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{Z}}

where
\overline{\mathbb{T}}=\GSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle s^{\pm 1}\rangle_{\leq 1}
denotes the overconvergent unit torus. In this case, for any
\varepsilon>0
, the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood
of
Z
in
X
is the closed substack
Z_{\varepsilon}\subseteq X
defined by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\varepsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon)/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\;,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{Z}}

where
\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon)\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{1}
denotes the overconvergent disk of radius
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
X
be a Gelfand stack and assume that
X
is nicely coverable. If
Z\subseteq X
is an overconvergent normed divisor, pullback induces an equivalence of categories

\Perf(Z)\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon})\;.

Proof.

Write
Z_{0}\coloneqq Z
for simplicity, i.e.
Z_{\varepsilon}=Z
if
\varepsilon=0
. Let
\GSpec A_{\bullet}\rightarrow X
be a nice hypercover and choose
n\geq 0
such that all
A_{\bullet}
are
n
-truncated. Then first observe that
X
is quasi-compact by assumption and hence the map
X\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}
classifying
Z
factors through
(\overline{\mathbb{D}}(r)/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}
for some
r>0
. Since the maps
\overline{\mathbb{D}}(\varepsilon)\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}(r)
are affine and have flat dimension at most
1
for all
0\leq\varepsilon\leq r
by
2.14
, base changing the given nice hypercover of
X
to
Z_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\geq 0
yields corresponding nice hypercovers

U_{\bullet}^{(\varepsilon)}=\GSpec A_{\bullet}^{(\varepsilon)}\rightarrow Z_{\varepsilon}\;.

Now any perfect complex on
Z_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\geq 0
comes from a perfect complex over the animated ring
A_{0}^{(\varepsilon)}
and hence has bounded
\operatorname{Tor}
-amplitude, which reduces us to showing that

\Perf^{[a,b]}(Z)\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf^{[a,b]}(Z_{\varepsilon})

via pullback for all
a\leq b
. However, by descent, we have

\Perf^{[a,b]}(Z_{\varepsilon})\cong\Tot(\Perf^{[a,b]}(A_{\bullet}^{(\varepsilon)}))\cong\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{\Delta_{\leq N}}\Perf^{[a,b]}(A_{\bullet}^{(\varepsilon)})

for some
N\geq 0
which only depends on
a,b
and
n
since all the
A_{\bullet}^{(\varepsilon)}
are
n
-truncated; here,
\Delta_{\leq N}
denotes the full subcategory of the simplex category
\Delta
spanned by the objects
\{0<1<\dots<i\}
with
i\leq N
. As filtered colimits commute with finite limits, we are then reduced to proving

\Perf^{[a,b]}(A_{i}^{(0)})\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf^{[a,b]}(A_{i}^{(\varepsilon)})

for all
a\leq b
and
i\geq 0
. However, note that
A_{i}^{(0)}=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}A_{i}^{(\varepsilon)}
by construction and hence the claim follows from
[
52
, Tag 0BC7]
.
∎

In applications, we will actually make use of the following easy consequence of
2.18
:

Corollary 2.19
.

Let
X
be a nicely coverable Gelfand stack and
Z\subseteq X
an overconvergent normed divisor. Then we have

\Perf(X)\cong\Perf(Z)\times_{\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z)}\Perf(X\setminus Z)

via pullback. Here,
X\setminus Z
is defined by the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\setminus Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})^{\mathrm{dR}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{Z}}

and
Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z
is the base change of
X\setminus Z
along
Z_{\varepsilon}\subseteq X
.

Proof.

Since filtered colimits commute with finite limits, the previous lemma yields

\begin{split}\Perf(Z)\times_{\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z)}\Perf(X\setminus Z)&\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon})\times_{\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z)}\Perf(X\setminus Z)\\
&\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}(\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon})\times_{\Perf(Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z)}\Perf(X\setminus Z))\\
&\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf(X)=\Perf(X)\;,\end{split}

where the penultimate step is due to the fact that
Z_{\varepsilon}
and
X\setminus Z
form a cover of
X
for any
\varepsilon>0
and intersect in
Z_{\varepsilon}\setminus Z
.
∎

2.4.
The Rees equivalence

Recall the classical Rees equivalence: Working in stacks on discrete static
A
-algebras for the fpqc topology, there is an equivalence

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{alg}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{alg}})\cong\Fun((\mathbb{Z},\geq),\mathcal{D}(A))=\mathcal{DF}(A)

between quasicoherent sheaves on
\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{alg}}/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{alg}}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{alg}}
acts on
\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{alg}}
by multiplication, and the filtered derived category of
A
; here we use the superscript
(-)^{\mathrm{alg}}
to distinguish the algebraic affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{alg}}
from the analytic affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}
in Gelfand stacks, and similarly for
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. This equivalence takes a filtered object
\Fil^{\bullet}M\in\mathcal{DF}(A)
to the graded
A[t]
-module

\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Fil^{i}F\cdot t^{-i}\;,

where
t
denotes the coordinate on
\mathbb{A}^{1,\mathrm{alg}}
. Even more fundamentally, there is an equivalence

\mathcal{D}(*/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{alg}})\cong\Fun(\mathbb{Z},\mathcal{D}(A))=\mathcal{D}_{\gr}(A)

between quasicoherent sheaves on
*/\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\mathrm{alg}}
and the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathcal{D}(A)
.

Our next objective is to prove a variant of this result in analytic geometry, i.e. in the setting of Gelfand stacks. We start by considering just the classifying stack of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
A
be any Gelfand ring. Then there is an equivalence between perfect complexes on
*/\mathbb{G}_{m}
over
A
and the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}_{\gr}(A)
consisting of objects
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M^{i}
satisfying the following two conditions:

(i)

Each
M^{i}
is a perfect complex over
A
.

(ii)

We have
M^{i}=0
for
i\gg 0
and
i\ll 0
.

The same is true for
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
in place of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

We first observe that the second part immediately follows from the first: Indeed, the map
*/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow*/\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a
(0,\infty)
-torsor by
2.22
below and thus pullback induces an equivalence on perfect complexes by
2.7
. Therefore, it suffices to prove the first assertion.

Now note that
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is suave and hence
[
39
, Prop. 3.1.27.(1)]
yields an identification

\mathcal{D}(\GSpec A/\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\Mod_{f^{!}f_{!}1}(\mathcal{D}(A))

via
f^{!}
, where
f:\GSpec A\rightarrow\GSpec A/\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the canonical map. Note that
f^{!}\cong f^{*}[1]
as the dualising sheaf of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is
1[1]
and so we also obtain an equivalence as above if we use
f^{*}
instead of
f^{!}
. By a direct computation, one sees that

f^{!}f_{!}1\cong A\{x^{-1}\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x\}^{\dagger}

with the multiplicative structure on the right-hand side being coefficient-wise multiplication.

Now given any
M\in\Perf(\GSpec A/\mathbb{G}_{m})
, the actions of
x^{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
yield pairwise orthogonal idempotent endomorphisms of the underlying
A
-module of
M
, which we will also denote by
M
by abuse of notation. Denoting the retracts of
M
corresponding to these idempotents by
M^{i}
, we claim that only finitely many of the
M^{i}
are nonzero. Once we know this, the fact that the sum of all
x^{i}
acts by the identity on
M
will imply that
M\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}M^{i}
, as desired.

To prove the remaining claim, first note that each
M^{i}
is a perfect complex as well (being a retract of
M
) and hence equivalent to a perfect complex over the discrete ring
A(*)
by
2.3
, and similarly for
M
itself. Then it suffices to show the statement we claim locally on
\Spec A(*)
: Indeed, the support of each
M^{i}
will be closed and
\Spec A(*)
is quasicompact. By the same argument, we may even reduce to checking the claim on stalks and then further to checking it on residue fields by Nakayama’s lemma. Finally, over any residue field
K
, we are in the following situation: we are given a perfect complex
M
over
K
and a chain

\dots\rightarrow\bigoplus_{|i|\leq n}M^{i}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{|i|\leq n+1}M^{i}\rightarrow\dots

of direct summands of
M
. Since the cohomology groups of
M
must be finite-dimensional and only finitely many of them can be nonzero, this chain must become stationary and hence
M^{i}=0
for
|i|\gg 0
.

The above discussion proves that we have a functor from the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}_{\gr}(A)
described in the statement to
\mathcal{D}(*/\mathbb{G}_{m})
and that it is essentially surjective. To see full faithfulness, note that the functor is symmetric monoidal and all objects in question are dualisable, hence it suffices to show that
\RHom
from the unit is preserved, for which we have to check that, in the above notation

\RHom_{A\{x^{-1}\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x\}^{\dagger}}\left(A,\bigoplus_{i}M^{i}\right)=M^{0}\;,

where
A
has degree zero. However, note that
A
is a retract of
A\{x^{-1}\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x\}^{\dagger}
with the retraction given by
x^{i}\mapsto 0
for all
i\neq 0
, i.e.
A
is a finite projective module over
A\{x^{-1}\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x\}^{\dagger}
, and then the claim follows.
∎

Before we can move on to the case of
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
, let us state the following preparatory lemma.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
A
be any Gelfand ring. Then pullback along the canonical map

\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}

induces an equivalence on perfect complexes.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will suppress the base
\GSpec A
from the notation for clarity. To begin, note that there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}

and we will show that pullback along the bottom map induces an equivalence on perfect complexes by proving the same for all the other maps in the diagram. Indeed, for the vertical maps, this just follows from the fact that they are torsors for
\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cong(0,\infty)
using
2.7
.

For the top horizontal map, we first observe that

\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\coprod_{\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{\times}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}}\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}

by a variant of
2.8
, where
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the overconvergent unit disk and
\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{\times}
is its punctured analogue. As
\overline{\mathbb{D}}^{\times}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cong(0,1]
and
\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cong(0,\infty)
, another application of
2.7
shows that pullback along the canonical map

\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}

induces an equivalence on perfect complexes. Finally, to see that pullback along
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}
induces an equivalence on perfect complexes, we use
2.19
: The stack
\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}
is nicely coverable (use e.g. the Čech nerve of the surjection
\overline{\mathbb{D}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}
) and applying loc. cit. to the overconvergent normed divisor
\{|t|=0\}
, where
t
denotes the coordinate on
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, we obtain

\Perf(\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})\cong\Perf(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})\times_{\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{\varepsilon>0}\Perf((0,\varepsilon])}\Perf((0,1])\cong\Perf(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\overline{\mathbb{T}})\;,

where the last equivalence is due to another application of
2.7
.
∎

The following statement was used in the proof:

Lemma 2.22
.

The norm map induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\cong(0,\infty)
.

Proof.

The proof is along the lines of the proofs of
[
46
, Prop. II.1.4, Thm. II.3.1]
. First, recall from
[
46
, Prop. II.1.3]
that a map
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
is equivalent to a collection
\{A_{Z}\}_{Z}
of idempotent algebras in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{G}_{m})
which become simultaneously connective after pullback along some
!
-cover of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where the index
Z
runs over closed subsets of
(0,\infty)
and we demand that the association
Z\mapsto A_{Z}
sends limits to colimits and finite unions to limits. In our case, it suffices for
Z
to run over closed intervals
[a,b]
and then the map in question is induced by sending
[a,b]
to the algebra of functions on the overconvergent torus
\overline{\mathbb{T}}(a,b)
of inner radius
a
and outer radius
b
. Note that the map
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
indeed factors through the quotient of the source by
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
.

We now first check that, for all
a\leq b
, the map
\overline{\mathbb{T}}(a,b)\rightarrow[a,b]
obtained in this way is surjective; since the closed intervals
[a,b]
jointly cover
(0,\infty)
, this will imply surjectivity of
\mathbb{G}_{m}/\overline{\mathbb{T}}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
. For this, note that there is a surjection
S\rightarrow[a,b]
from the Cantor set
S=\operatornamewithlimits{lim}_{n}\{0,1\}^{n}
onto
[a,b]
such that the image of each
s_{n}\times_{S_{n}}S
in
[a,b]
for
s_{n}\in S_{n}=\{0,1\}^{n}
is given by a closed interval of length
2^{-n}(b-a)
and it will be enough to show that the base change of
\overline{\mathbb{T}}(a,b)\rightarrow[a,b]
along
S\rightarrow[a,b]
is surjective. To this end, writing

X_{n}\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{T}}(a,b)\times_{[a,b]}\bigsqcup_{s_{n}\in S_{n}}(s_{n}\times_{S_{n}}S)\;,

we see that
X_{n}
is a disjoint union of
2^{n}
overconvergent tori and

X\coloneqq\overline{\mathbb{T}}(a,b)\times_{[a,b]}S

is affine with coordinate ring
\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{n}\mathcal{O}(X_{n})
. Thus, we are done once we show that

\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{S_{n}}=C^{\mathrm{sm}}(S,\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{n}\mathcal{O}(X_{n})

is descendable. However, by
[
35
, Prop. 2.7.2]
, this follows from the fact that each map
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{S_{n}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(X_{n})
has a section as modules and is thus descendable of index
1
.

Having established surjectivity of
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow(0,\infty)
, it suffices to check that the fibres of this map are
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
-torsors. However, note that the map in question is even a map of group stacks and, by definition, the preimage of
1\in(0,\infty)
is given by
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
, which yields the claim.
∎

Finally, we can prove the analytic version of the Rees equivalence we will need.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
A
be any Gelfand ring. Then there is an equivalence between perfect complexes on
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
over
A
and the full subcategory of
\mathcal{DF}(A)
consisting of objects
\Fil^{\bullet}M
satisfying the following two conditions:

(i)

Each
\Fil^{i}M
is a perfect complex over
A
.

(ii)

We have
\Fil^{i}M=0
for
i\gg 0
and
\Fil^{i}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i-1}M
is an isomorphism for
i\ll 0
.

Proof.

By
2.21
, it suffices to prove the analogous characterisation for perfect complexes on
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
instead. As in the proof of
2.20
, the fact that
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is suave with dualising sheaf
1[1]
shows that

(2.4.1)

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A)\cong\mathcal{D}(A\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger})

via
f^{*}
, where the multiplication on the
A
-algebra
A\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger}
is noncommutative and governed by the relations

tx^{i}=x^{i+1}t\;,\hskip 8.5359ptx^{i}x^{j}=\delta_{ij}x^{i}\;,\hskip 8.5359ptt^{i}t^{j}=t^{i+j}

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta. Note that the derived category on the right-hand side of (
2.4.1
) refers to the derived category of left modules.

Now given any
M\in\Perf(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A)
, the actions of
x^{-i}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
again yield pairwise orthogonal idempotent endomorphisms of the underlying
A
-module of
M
, which we will also denote by
M
by abuse of notation. Denoting the retracts corresponding to these idempotents by
\Fil^{i}M
, note that the action of
t
induces maps
\Fil^{i}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i-1}M
. We claim the following:

(i)

The pushforward of
M
to
\GSpec A
is given by
\Fil^{0}M
.

(ii)

The maps
\Fil^{i}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i-1}M
are isomorphisms for
i\ll 0
.

(iii)

We have
\Fil^{i}M=0
for
i\gg 0
.

(iv)

There is an isomorphism
(\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Fil^{i}M\cdot t^{-i})\otimes_{A[t]}A\{t\}^{\dagger}\cong M
.

If we can prove this, we are done: Any filtered
A
-module satisfying the assumptions of the proposition will yield a perfect complex on
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A
via the formula (iv) and then we know that this functor is essentially surjective by (iv) and fully faithful by (i).

To prove (i), note that the unit on
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A
corresponds to
A\{t\}^{\dagger}
with the left
A\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger}
-module structure given by

x^{i}\cdot t^{j}=\delta_{ij}t^{j}\;,\hskip 8.5359ptt^{i}\cdot t^{j}=t^{i+j}

and our task is to compute the
\RHom
from
A\{t\}^{\dagger}
to
M
. However, note that
A\{t\}^{\dagger}
is actually a retract of
A\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger}
and thus finite projective: Indeed, the inclusion and retraction maps are given by

\displaystyle A\{t\}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\rightarrow A\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger}\;,\hskip 14.22636ptA\{x^{-1},t\}^{\dagger}\oplus xA\{x,t\}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\rightarrow A\{t\}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle t^{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto x^{i}t^{i}

\displaystyle\mathllap{x^{i}t^{j}}\mapsto t^{i}\;.

Now the claim follows upon noticing that the image of
1\in A\{t\}^{\dagger}
already determines the map
A\{t\}^{\dagger}\rightarrow M
by linearity and that
1
is fixed by
x^{0}
, hence has to land in
\Fil^{0}M
.

For (ii), we note that pulling back
M
along
\GSpec A/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A
yields a perfect complex and that the
M^{i}
occurring in the proof of
2.20
are precisely the associated gradeds of the filtration
\Fil^{i}M
up to a sign. As only finitely many
M^{i}
are nonzero, we conclude that
\Fil^{i}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i-1}M
is an isomorphism for
|i|\gg 0
.

Finally, we prove (iii) and (iv) at once. By the previous paragraph, we know that there is some
N\geq 0
such that
\Fil^{i+1}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i}M
is an isomorphism for all
i\geq N
. Moreover, by (i),
2.21
and
2.24
below, we know that
\Fil^{0}M
is a perfect complex over
A
. As the argument from the previous paragraph also shows that all associated gradeds of the filtration are perfect complexes over
A
, this implies that all
\Fil^{i}M
are perfect complexes over
A
by induction. Now
(\bigoplus_{i\leq N}\Fil^{i}M\cdot t^{-i})\otimes_{A[t]}A\{t\}^{\dagger}
defines a perfect complex on
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A
: Indeed, choosing
n\ll 0
such that
\Fil^{i}M\rightarrow\Fil^{i-1}M
is an isomorphism for all
i\leq n
, the complex we claim to be perfect receives a natural map from the evidently perfect complex
\Fil^{n}M\cdot t^{-n}\otimes_{A}A\{t\}^{\dagger}
and the cofibre of this map is given by
\bigoplus_{n<i\leq N}\Fil^{i}M\cdot t^{-i}
, which is also perfect since each
\Fil^{i}M
is a perfect complex over
A
. Moreover,
M
receives a natural map

\left(\bigoplus_{i\leq N}\Fil^{i}M\cdot t^{-i}\right)\otimes_{A[t]}A\{t\}^{\dagger}\rightarrow M

and since both source and target are perfect over
A\{t\}^{\dagger}
, checking if this is an isomorphism may be done as modules over
A\{t\}^{\dagger}(*)
, and after modding out the Jacobson radical (by Nakayama’s lemma). However, since
t
is
\dagger
-nilpotent in
A\{t\}^{\dagger}
, it is in the Jacobson radical of
A\{t\}^{\dagger}(*)
and hence we may in particular check the above isomorphism after modding out
t
. In other words, we may check the above isomorphism after pullback to
\GSpec A/\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A
, but there it follows from the proof of
2.20
above by our choice of
N
. We conclude that
\Fil^{i}M=0
for
i>N
, proving (iii), and then the above isomorphism is exactly (iv), so we are done.
∎

The following statement was used in the proof:

Lemma 2.24
.

For any Gelfand ring
A
, pushforward along
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GSpec A\rightarrow\GSpec A
preserves perfect complexes.

Proof.

We first note that
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
